- Annex III: Environmental impact assessment report (template provided)
- Annex IV: Defined budget plan (template provided)
- Annex V: Implementation timeline including the programme structure (template provided)
  - EIA document corresponding to the EIA category (A, B or C or if 1/2 or 3/5):
    - Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or
    - Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP)

## H2 - Other annexes as applicable:
- Annex VII: Summary of consultation and stakeholder engagement plan
- Annex VIII: Gender assessment and project programme level action plan (template provided)
- Annex IX: Legal-the-difference regulation, taxes and insurance
- Annex X: Financial risk management plan (template provided)
- Annex XI: AEI for equity (template provided)
- Annex XII: Co-financing commitment letter (applicable) (template provided)

NO-OBJECTON LETTER IN RESPECT OF THE FUNDING PROPOSAL TITLED "SCALING UP INTEGRATED LAND USE MANAGEMENT FOR SUSTAINABLE AGRICULTURE AND FORESTRY IN VULNERABLE REGIONS OF SOUTH AFRICA" SUBMITTED BY THE SOUTH AFRICAN NATIONAL DOUGLAS INITIATIVE

The funding proposal titled "Scaling up Integrated Land Use Management for Sustainable Agriculture and Forestry in Vulnerable Regions of South Africa" submitted by the South African National Douglas Initiative (SANDI) to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) is under consideration.

The Government of the Republic of Korea, represented by the Ministry of Environment (MOE), the national designated authority of the Republic of Korea, hereby provides its no-objection letter in respect of the funding proposal titled "Scaling up Integrated Land Use Management for Sustainable Agriculture and Forestry in Vulnerable Regions of South Africa" submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) by the South African National Douglas Initiative (SANDI).

Pursuant to Green Climate Fund (GCF) Decisions B.09/15, B.37/2 and B.41/12, the content of which is hereby incorporated by reference, the no-objection letter issued by the national designated authority of the Republic of Korea in respect of the funding proposal is hereby annexed.

By communicating the MOE's no-objection letter, it is implied that:

- The Government of the Republic of Korea does not object to the submission and consideration of the funding proposal; and
- The funding proposal is in conformity with the national policies, strategies and plans of the Republic of Korea.

Please note that this no-objection letter does not constitute an endorsement or approval of the funding proposal by the Government of the Republic of Korea. Any questions regarding the funding proposal should be directed to the South African National Douglas Initiative (SANDI).

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP267

**Proposal name:** Scaling up sustainable bioenergy approaches to managing invasive alien plants in South Africa (Bio-ERP)

**Accredited entity:** South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI)

**Country:** South Africa

**Project size:** Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
This is the first project submitted by the South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI) to the GEF since its approval. The funding proposal originates from the Bio-ERP project, which has been implemented in South Africa and Mozambique. The project builds on the experience of SANBI and its partners in managing invasive alien plants through sustainable bioenergy approaches. The project is aligned with the objectives of the National Development Plan (NDP) and the Integrated Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (ICCSAP). It also contributes to the implementation of the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 7 (clean energy), SDG 13 (climate action), and SDG 15 (life on land).

### Points of concern
The success of the project relies on strong collaboration with multiple national, provincial, and local government actors. However, while engaging multiple partners can enhance project sustainability, it also presents valuable challenges in terms of coordination and communication.

The “Scaling up sustainable bioenergy approaches to managing invasive alien plants in South Africa” (Bio-ERP) project has been implemented in several regions of South Africa (KwaZulu-Natal, Eastern Cape, Free State, Northern Cape, Western Cape). The project aims to address the challenge of invasive alien plant management by combining bioenergy conservation and sustainable development approaches. The project has demonstrated significant achievements in terms of invasive alien plant control and bioenergy production.

The scaling-up approach proposed in this project builds on the success achieved during the Bio-ERP project implementation phase, which was implemented as a pilot project in several regions of South Africa (KwaZulu-Natal, Eastern Cape, Free State, Northern Cape, Western Cape). The project aims to address the challenge of invasive alien plant management by combining bioenergy conservation and sustainable development approaches. The project has demonstrated significant achievements in terms of invasive alien plant control and bioenergy production.

The scaling-up approach proposed in this project builds on the success achieved during the Bio-ERP project implementation phase, which was implemented as a pilot project in several regions of South Africa (KwaZulu-Natal, Eastern Cape, Free State, Northern Cape, Western Cape). The project aims to address the challenge of invasive alien plant management by combining bioenergy conservation and sustainable development approaches. The project has demonstrated significant achievements in terms of invasive alien plant control and bioenergy production.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed below that agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), subject to the conditions outlined in the AE’s funding agreement. GEF/42/SA/02.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. South Africa faces significant climate change challenges, including increased temperatures, altered rainfall patterns and more frequent extreme weather events. These changes have implications for water resources management, agriculture, biodiversity, human health, infrastructure, tourism, and other sectors. The country has experienced multiple droughts in recent years, which have had severe impacts on the agricultural sector and water availability. These hazards have also been recurring, causing significant economic losses.

2. Climate change has been further exacerbated by human activities such as overexploiting the environment, deforestation, and land degradation. This has led to increased greenhouse gas emissions, contributing to global warming and climate change. The country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to its high dependence on agriculture and water resources.

3. To address these challenges, EIAs and environmental impact assessments (EIAs) are required for all new development projects. However, the implementation of these measures has been slow and inconsistent. Additionally, there is a lack of public awareness and understanding of climate change and its impacts.

To address these challenges, EIAs and environmental impact assessments (EIAs) are required for all new development projects. However, the implementation of these measures has been slow and inconsistent. Additionally, there is a lack of public awareness and understanding of climate change and its impacts.

# Project management (Direct, USD 4.4 million; GEF, USD 1.9 million)

Project management and monitoring components: (Expenditure, USD 2.4 million; GEF cost, USD 0.4 million)

The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MoEF) in collaboration with state governments and local communities. It will also retain final authority to ensure that the project is implemented according to the approved plan.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential

The project will directly benefit 50,000 community members from vulnerable groups by providing them with access to improved livelihoods and income opportunities through sustainable forest management practices. The project will also contribute to the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services in the region.

The project will be implemented in collaboration with local communities and other stakeholders. It will also retain final authority to ensure that the project is implemented according to the approved plan.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project (GEF) aims to generate impact beyond the four targeted sites by promoting sustainable forest management practices at the landscape level. This will contribute to community-based natural resource management, to promote ecosystem functionality. This innovation will help improve livelihoods.

The project will demonstrate the ecological and financial value of adopting sustainable forest management practices. It will also provide a model for other countries in the region that are seeking to adopt similar approaches. The project will also contribute to the development of new technologies and methods for sustainable forest management.

**Page 86**

# Sustainable development potential

## Eco-DRIVE project overview

The Eco-DRIVE project aims to promote gender equality and empower women by ensuring that women are not only beneficiaries but also active participants in the project's activities. The project will help address gender-based violence, promote women's empowerment, and enhance their participation in decision-making processes at all levels.

### a) Gender mainstreaming

In line with SDG 5 (Gender equality), the Eco-DRIVE project supports directly the achievement of this goal by promoting gender equality through various activities. The project will help address gender-based violence, promote women's empowerment, and enhance their participation in decision-making processes at all levels.

### b) Women's economic empowerment

The Eco-DRIVE project also aims to support SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth). The project focuses on creating decent job opportunities for women, particularly in the agricultural sector. This will help improve the livelihoods of rural communities by providing them with sustainable income sources.

## Needs of the recipient

### a) Capacity building

The Eco-DRIVE project aims to build the capacity of local communities through various training programs and workshops. These activities will focus on environmental management, climate change adaptation, and ecosystem valuation. A project like the Eco-DRIVE can help address these needs by providing the necessary skills and knowledge for sustainable development.

### b) Long-term engagement

Long-term engagement is crucial for the success of the Eco-DRIVE project. The project aims to create lasting impacts in the communities it serves. This includes promoting sustainable practices, improving livelihoods, and ensuring that the benefits of the project are sustained over time. The project will also focus on creating local ownership and ensuring that the community is actively involved in the implementation and monitoring of the project activities.

## Country oversight

### a) Environmental protection

The Eco-DRIVE project aligns closely with national and regional policies, particularly in environmental protection, climate change mitigation, disaster risk reduction, and climate-resilient natural resources management. The project will work to enhance these efforts by providing additional support and resources to the communities it serves.

# Women and Maternal Health

Women and maternal health pages from participation in local planning and risk management efforts. These goals are accomplished by standardized procedures, district nurses/medical officers link to community-based health workers, and the development of a referral system that ensures access to quality care for all women. The program also aims to prevent maternal mortality and morbidity through improved antenatal care, skilled attendance at delivery, and postnatal care.

## Goals

The goals of action plan define activities, indicators, targets for women's participation in local planning and risk management efforts. These include:

- Improving access to quality health services.
- Increasing the number of women who receive antenatal care.
- Reducing maternal mortality and morbidity rates.
- Ensuring that all women have access to skilled birth attendants.

The goals also include indicators such as the percentage of women who receive antenatal care, the percentage of deliveries attended by skilled personnel, and the percentage of women who receive postnatal care. The goals are designed to be achievable within a specified time frame and with available resources.

- Improving access to skilled birth attendants.

## Legal assessment

### Accreditation Entity Agreement

The Accreditation Entity Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 12 July 2017 (the "AAE"). Later amendments to the AAE were signed on 3 October 2018 and 6 February 2019. The AAE is a legally binding agreement between the Accreditation Entity and the Accredited Entity. The AAE provides that:

- The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion confirming that it has complied with all applicable laws in relation to its accreditation activities.
- The proposed project will be implemented by the Republic of South Africa, contrary to the provisions of the AAE which requires that the GEF not promote projects in countries where the GEF has not promoted agencies. This is a minor issue and does not affect the overall assessment of the country's ability to handle future GEF projects.
- The Accreditation Entity has not provided a legal opinion confirming that it has complied with all applicable laws in relation to its accreditation activities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP267

## Proposed name
Scaling up community-based approaches to managing wildlife-induced disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa (Version 1)

## Recommended name
South African National Biodiversity Insurer (SANBI)

## Country
South Africa

## Project type
New

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### i. Summary of the project
South Africa faces multiple, interconnected challenges driven by climate change, human-wildlife conflict, and biodiversity loss. The increasing frequency and severity of droughts, fires, rainfall events, and wildfires across the country have increased markedly in recent years. These changes have been exacerbated by accelerated land-use change and infrastructure development, particularly in areas adjacent to protected areas and the boundaries of South Africa’s national parks.

The project intends to address these challenges through a proposed financial risk management approach that adapts to climate change and integrates biodiversity conservation into disaster risk reduction strategies. The project aims to develop a climate-resilient insurance product for wildlife-induced disasters that can be scaled up nationally, regionally, or locally as needed. The project will also provide technical assistance to communities at risk of wildlife-induced disasters.

### ii. Objectives
The adaptation-focused project contributes to adaptation results area 1 (Minimize loss of life and assets due to climate change) through the following objectives:
- Objective 1: The implementation of an insurance product that reduces the impact of wildlife-induced disasters on vulnerable communities.
- Objective 2: The establishment of a risk management framework for wildlife-induced disasters in South Africa.

### iii. Outputs
The project will contribute to the following outputs:
- Output 1: The creation of an insurance product that reduces disaster risk reduction into transboundary disaster preparedness and response.
- Output 2: The development of a risk management framework for wildlife-induced disasters.
- Output 3: The establishment of an enabling environment for investment in ecosystem-based adaptation and knowledge management.

### iv. Relevance
The project is relevant to the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) because it addresses climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction, and biodiversity conservation. The project also contributes to the GCF’s strategic objective of promoting a paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways.

### v. Co-benefits
The project will contribute to the following co-benefits:
- Co-benefit 1: The creation of an enabling environment for investment in ecosystem-based adaptation.
- Co-benefit 2: The establishment of a risk management framework for wildlife-induced disasters.
- Co-benefit 3: The promotion of biodiversity conservation and sustainable land-use practices.

### vi. Additionality
The project is additional because it addresses the unique challenges faced by South Africa in managing wildlife-induced disaster risks. The project also builds on existing efforts to develop climate-resilient insurance products and risk management frameworks for wildlife-induced disasters.

### vii. Efficiency
The project is efficient because it leverages existing infrastructure, expertise, and partnerships to implement an innovative insurance product that reduces disaster risk reduction into transboundary disaster preparedness and response. The project also builds on existing efforts to develop climate-resilient insurance products and risk management frameworks for wildlife-induced disasters.

### viii. Sustainability
The project is sustainable because it establishes a long-term risk management framework for wildlife-induced disasters in South Africa. The project also promotes biodiversity conservation and sustainable land-use practices, which are essential for long-term sustainability.

# Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-sensitive disease risks to waterfowl regions of South Africa (Eco-DBR)

## Figure caption

Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-sensitive disease risks to waterfowl regions of South Africa (Eco-DBR) is a project funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The project is implemented through the South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI), in collaboration with the Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE), and the South African Wildlife College Association (SAWCA).

## Project overview

The Eco-DBR project aims to enhance the resilience of waterfowl regions to climate change by implementing ecosystem-based approaches. The project will be implemented over a period of eight years, from 2021 to 2029.

### Key objectives

- **Objective 1:** Enhance the resilience of waterfowl regions to climate change through ecosystem-based approaches.
- **Objective 2:** Strengthen national and local capacities for integrated management of waterfowl regions.
- **Objective 3:** Improve the livelihoods of communities dependent on waterfowl regions.

### Implementation strategy

The Eco-DBR project will be implemented in four district municipalities, namely Ethekwini, Msunduzi, uMkhomazi, and uMngeni. These areas are affected by climate change and require integrated management to enhance their resilience. The project will also involve a significant community engagement component.

### Funding

The Eco-DBR project, which requires a USD 8,164,179 grant from GEF, is to be implemented over a period of eight years, from 2021 to 2029. This funding is provided by the Global Environment Facility (GEF) through the South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI), contribution valued at USD 3,785,469.

### Collaborators

- **South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI):** Contribution valued at USD 3,785,469.
- **Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE)**
- **South African Wildlife College Association (SAWCA):** Contribution valued at USD 1,772.

### Implementation timeline

The project will be implemented in two phases:

- **Phase I:** 2021 to 2023
- **Phase II:** 2024 to 2029

### Community engagement

Community engagement is a crucial aspect of the project, which will be implemented in collaboration with a range of stakeholders. This includes local communities, government agencies, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs). The project aims to enhance community participation in integrated management and will implement activities under outcome 1. SAWCA and the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) will play key roles in this engagement.

### Project components

The Eco-DBR project includes several key components:

- **Component 1:** Enhancing resilience through ecosystem-based approaches.
- **Component 2:** Strengthening national and local capacities for integrated management of waterfowl regions.
- **Component 3:** Improving livelihoods of communities dependent on waterfowl regions.

### Implementation approach

The Eco-DBR project will be implemented in four district municipalities, namely Ethekwini, Msunduzi, uMkhomazi, and uMngeni. These areas are affected by climate change and require integrated management to enhance their resilience. The project will also involve a significant community engagement component.

### Funding breakdown

The Eco-DBR project requires a total funding of USD 8,164,179 from GEF over eight years. This includes contributions from SANBI valued at USD 3,785,469 and other stakeholders.

### 1.1 Impact potential:

Within Project I, communities in Alfred Nzo, Eastern Cape, Northern Cape, North West, Mpumalanga and Sekhukhune are anticipated to benefit from subnational, mainstreamed and/or sustainable disaster risk management (DRM) interventions. These interventions will be implemented through the Department of Cooperative Governance (DCOG), which has control over both environmental changes in governance, remuneration with subgrantees and the provision of technical support. The project is expected to have a positive impact on households by enhancing their exposure to climate change risks.

As per Area 21, district municipalities are identified as key partners within the specific targeted provinces. These local disaster management authorities will be better able to manage disaster risk at the local level through improved coordination and collaboration with other stakeholders in the area. The project is expected to have a positive impact on households by enhancing their exposure to climate change risks.

The project will also benefit from the ability of district municipalities to better engage with the majority of climate change-induced disasters, floods and wildfires as a result of scaling up the project.

# Green Book Development by the Green Book Consortium

## Introduction

The Green Book supports change adaptation and resilience building through a framework that enables individuals, communities, and organizations to be more resilient in the face of climate-related disasters and other challenges. The Green Book provides a comprehensive approach to understanding and addressing the impacts of climate change on health and well-being.

### Key Features

- **Comprehensive Framework**: The Green Book offers a holistic approach to climate resilience by integrating various aspects such as public health, environmental sustainability, and social equity.
- **Adaptable Strategies**: It includes strategies that can be tailored to specific contexts and needs, making it versatile for different regions and communities.
- **Community Engagement**: Emphasizes the importance of community involvement in developing and implementing climate resilience plans.

## Activities

### Activity 2.1.4: Green Book Development

The Green Book Consortium is working on the development of the Green Book. This involves several steps:

1. **Consultation and Feedback**: Engaging with stakeholders to gather insights and feedback.
2. **Drafting and Review**: Creating a draft version of the book, followed by multiple rounds of review and revision.
3. **Pilot Testing**: Implementing pilot projects in various settings to test the applicability and effectiveness of the strategies outlined.

### SAMEL

SAMEL is also partnering with academic institutions to conduct deep research and develop case studies that will inform the development of the Green Book. This collaboration aims to enhance the practical application of the book's recommendations through real-world examples and best practices.

## Conclusion

The Green Book Development by the Green Book Consortium is a significant step towards building climate resilience. By integrating various aspects of public health, environmental sustainability, and community engagement, the Green Book provides a comprehensive framework for addressing the challenges posed by climate change.

# Sustainable development

The programme aims to contribute to sustainable development through Goals (SDGs), specifically:

- SDG 1: The Poverty,
- SDG 2: (Good Health),
- SDG 3: (Good Health and Well-being),
- SDG 5: (Gender Equality),
- SDG 8: (Decent Work and Economic Growth).

Two main targets are specified:

### Target 1
Reduce the global maternal mortality ratio and preventable deaths for newborns and children under 5.

### Target 2
Ensure universal access to sexual and reproductive health, and family planning services by 2030.

## Goals

- **Goal 1:** End poverty in all its forms everywhere.
- **Goal 2:** End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition, and promote sustainable agriculture.
- **Goal 3:** Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages.
- **Goal 5:** Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls.

### Targets

1. By 2030, reduce the global maternal mortality ratio to less than 70 per 100,000 live births.
2. By 2030, end preventable deaths of newborns and children under 5, with all countries aiming to reduce neonatal mortality to at least as low as 12 per 1,000 live births and under-5 mortality to at least as low as 25 per 1,000 live births.
3. By 2030, end the epidemics of AIDS, tuberculosis, malaria and neglected tropical diseases and combat hepatitis, water-borne diseases and other communicable diseases.

### Economic development

The biological revolution activities seek to develop new technologies that can be used in the health sector. These include:

- **Target 1:** Reduce the global maternal mortality ratio to less than 70 per 100,000 live births.
- **Target 2:** End preventable deaths of newborns and children under 5, with all countries aiming to reduce neonatal mortality to at least as low as 12 per 1,000 live births and under-5 mortality to at least as low as 25 per 1,000 live births.
- **Target 3:** End the epidemics of AIDS, tuberculosis, malaria and neglected tropical diseases and combat hepatitis, water-borne diseases and other communicable diseases.

- **Target 3:** End the epidemics of AIDS, tuberculosis, malaria and neglected tropical diseases and combat hepatitis, water-borne diseases and other communicable diseases.

- **Target 3:** End the epidemics of AIDS, tuberculosis, malaria and neglected tropical diseases and combat hepatitis, water-borne diseases and other communicable diseases.

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: High

1. As highlighted above, South Africa faces multiple interconnected challenges given by:
   - Climate change
   - Gender inequality
   - Poverty
   - Violence
The impacts of climate change are being felt across the country, with increasing frequency and severity of extreme weather events recorded in recent decades.

2. The Global Adaptation Index (GAIN) currently ranks South Africa 95th out of 187 countries. Its particularly vulnerable to:
   - Droughts
   - Floods
   - Wildfires
These are the effects of climate change which plague South Africa severely.

3. Additionally, the country’s vulnerability is exacerbated by:
   - High levels of poverty
   - Inequality
   - Unemployment

## Country ownership

### Scale: High

1. Country ownership and domestic capacity to implement development programmes is a challenge for many countries. South Africa stands out as this programme has been developed with the government, private sector, and civil society.

2. The programme has been developed with stakeholders, particularly the Department of Environmental Affairs (DEA), which is responsible for environmental protection and policy implementation in the country.

3. The programme has been developed with the support of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF).

4. The global agencies involved in the initiative are financing and nationally led, ensuring that the programme is owned by South Africa.

5. The financial assistance provided to the country is for the implementation of the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

## Background

The impacts of climate change have been felt across South Africa with the frequency and severity of floods, droughts and wildfires increasing. Climate projections show that the trend including changes in intensity and unpredictability, will continue to increase over time. These changes are expected to impact on water availability, food security, health, and socio-economic development, as well as urban and rural livelihoods and infrastructure.

In response, South African Natural Disaster Management Authority (SANDMA) is preparing a full application with the associated budgeting documents to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) focal point to provide evidence-based approaches to climate change adaptation. The project will be implemented in the Eastern Cape Province of South Africa over five years and will include the following components: (i) development of a climate change risk assessment framework; (ii) development of an early warning system for drought, floods, and wildfires; (iii) development of a climate change adaptation plan; (iv) development of a climate change adaptation plan; (v) development of a climate change adaptation plan; (vi) development of a climate change adaptation plan; (vii) development of a climate change adaptation plan; (viii) development of a climate change adaptation plan.

## Gender Legislation and Institutional Frameworks

The legal framework in South Africa on women's empowerment and gender equality is enshrined in the Constitution, parliamentary debates of South Africa and international conventions. The government has developed policies and strategies to address gender inequality and promote women's rights. These include the following:

- The Department of Women, Youth and Persons with Disabilities Act (DWYPDA) is a crucial instrument in addressing gender-based violence.
- The Department of Social Development Act is an essential tool for promoting gender equality and women's empowerment.
- The Department of Health Act is a vital component in ensuring the health and well-being of women and children.
- The Department of Labour Act is a key legislation that promotes equal opportunities for women in the workplace.

The institutional framework includes the Department of Women, Youth and Persons with Disabilities, an inter-ministerial task team on gender-based violence, and the Commission for Gender Equality. These institutions work together to promote gender equality and address issues related to gender-based violence and discrimination against women and girls.

## Challenges

Despite significant progress in addressing gender inequality, challenges persist including gender-based violence, unequal access to education and employment, and discriminatory social norms, particularly affecting women in rural areas and poor communities. In response to the challenges, the government has implemented various policies and programs aimed at promoting gender equality and empowering women.

# country's commitment to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change: South Africa's progress towards achieving its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs)

South Africa has made significant strides in addressing climate change and promoting sustainable development. The country has committed to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 34% by 2025 and 42% by 2030, compared to business-as-usual scenarios. South Africa's efforts include the implementation of the National Climate Change Response Plan on Gender, Biodiversity, and Health, targeting gender equality through various initiatives for the Gender Equality Strategy.

## Overview of Gender Strategy in South Africa

In terms of demographic data, the population stands at 59 million, with females comprising 51%, based on the latest census. Female headed households increased to 40%, with notable variations among age groups. The crime and violence against women and children have been identified as critical issues.

South Africa's gender equality strategy focuses on empowering women through various initiatives such as:

- Gender mainstreaming in all sectors
- Promoting equal opportunities for women in the workplace
- Encouraging women's participation in decision-making processes

The country aims to promote political empowerment for women challenges in economic participation and opportunity.

Gender issues linked to access to financial resources, with women represented in the justice and environmental groups. The government has implemented various programs aimed at ensuring gender equality and promoting women's rights. High levels of poverty, health care accessibility, and violence against women are critical areas that need attention.

### South Africa's Gender Equality Strategy

South Africa's Gender Equality Strategy aims to address gender-based violence (GBV) and promote gender equality. The government has implemented various programs aimed at addressing GBV and promoting gender equality. High levels of poverty, health care accessibility, and violence against women are critical areas that need attention.

### Women's Status in the Energy-Driven Economy

The African Renewable Energy Initiative (AREI), Ethanol, and Sustainable Development Mechanism (SDM) present several types of challenges to South Africa's energy sector. Each of these sectors faces unique challenges related to gender equality and women's empowerment.

spiraling activists, peer-to-peer management and action to essential services, particularly in poorly planned urban areas, has been a key driver of the emergence of community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) plans. These plans are often integrated into broader spatial planning initiatives, including land use for the purpose, in addition, these plans can act as a foundation on which longer-term strategies for sustainable development and resilience building can be constructed. The CBDRM approach has been adopted by governments at various levels of administration and is increasingly recognized as an effective tool to enhance disaster risk reduction efforts. Although the approach has been widely adopted, challenges remain in its implementation.

In all of the DRMs reviewed, and other vulnerability studies for restricted areas, there are notable gaps in research across several domains. Furthermore, the adoption of CBDRM plans has been limited by a lack of understanding of the underlying causes of disaster risk and vulnerability. Additionally, there is a need to better understand the social, economic, and environmental factors that contribute to disaster risk and vulnerability. This includes an understanding of the root causes of disaster risk and vulnerability, as well as the impact of climate change on disaster risk.

Overall, there are several key areas where CBDRM can be improved. These include the development of more comprehensive plans, the integration of social, economic, and environmental factors into planning processes, and the use of participatory approaches to engage communities in the planning process. Additionally, there is a need for greater coordination between different levels of government and between different sectors.

Gender Analysis and Recommendations for the Gender Action Plan

The analysis conducted reveals that, despite progress, significant challenges remain in addressing gender equality in DRM. The lack of gender-specific data and indicators makes it difficult to assess the impact of DRM interventions on women and girls. Additionally, there is a need for greater engagement with local communities and for more inclusive planning processes.

To address these challenges, the following recommendations are made:

1. Integrate gender-specific data and indicators into DRM plans.
2. Engage local communities in the development of DRM plans.
3. Ensure that DRM interventions are designed to meet the needs of women and girls.
4. Promote greater coordination between different levels of government and between different sectors.

3. Ensure that DRM interventions are designed to meet the needs of women and girls.
4. Promote greater coordination between different levels of government and between different sectors.

The analysis conducted reveals that, despite progress, significant challenges remain in addressing gender equality in DRM. The lack of gender-specific data and indicators makes it difficult to assess the impact of DRM interventions on women and girls. Additionally, there is a need for greater engagement with local communities and for more inclusive planning processes.

To address these challenges, the following recommendations are made:

1. Integrate gender-specific data and indicators into DRM plans.
2. Engage local communities in the development of DRM plans.
3. Ensure that DRM interventions are designed to meet the needs of women and girls.
4. Promote greater coordination between different levels of government and between different sectors.

# LIST OF DEFINITIONS / CONCEPTS

For ease of reference, the following definitions and concepts are revised for the programme and understanding this document.

Gender refers to the socially constructed roles, behaviours, activities, and attributes that a given society considers appropriate for men, women, and others regardless of sex. Gender is usually determined by social norms and practices during early childhood and reinforced throughout life (UNICEF, 2016).

Gender identity refers to each person’s deeply-felt, inherent sense of being male, female or something else, which may or may not correspond to the sex assigned at birth following medical examination at the time of birth. Gender identity can be the same as or different from the sex assigned at birth.

Gender relations refer to dynamic social arrangements with hierarchical gender roles that influence attitudes and behaviours towards women, men, boys, girls, and others. Gender relations are shaped by cultural norms, values, beliefs, and practices. Gender relations are influenced by the economic, political, legal, social, and environmental contexts in which they occur.

Gender equality refers to the equal rights, responsibilities, and opportunities of women, men, girls, and boys across all sectors of society, including the family, community, and workplace (UN Women, 2019). Gender equality is not a women’s issue only. It requires the participation and engagement of everyone across individual, interpersonal, community, institutional, and societal levels.

Sex refers to biological attributes in humans and animals. It is primarily associated with physical and physiological features (WHO, 2018).

Gender mainstreaming is the process of making the concerns and experiences of women as well as men an integral part of the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of policies and programmes so that both women and men benefit and an equitable balance is achieved. Gender mainstreaming is not to be confused with affirmative action (UN Women, 2019).

Gender Responsive Budgeting is a process of identifying the differential needs and priorities of women and men in order to make budgetary allocations that are sensitive to gender issues. It aims to ensure that public and private resources are used efficiently, effectively, equitably, transparently, and accountably (UN Women, 2019).

Gender Mainstreaming Plus refers to the integration of gender analysis into policy-making processes in order to address the root causes of gender inequality. Gender mainstreaming plus includes a variety of gender-sensitive actions such as action plans, frameworks, indicators, and mechanisms to be embedded by groups implementing policies (Austrian Development Bank, 2015).

Gender Algebra refers to the far-reaching implications and responsibilities between women and men across all sectors of society. It is a concept that encompasses the need for gender equality in all aspects of life including education, employment, health, family, and community.

See references to a list of biological attributes in humans and animals. It is primarily associated with physical and physiological features (WHO, 2018).

# Gender Sensitivity

Gender sensitivity refers to the awareness, understanding, and consideration of the needs, expectations, and experiences of individuals based on their gender identity. It is a crucial aspect of promoting equality and fairness in various contexts.

## Gender Sensitization Approach

Aims to promote equality and address issues related to gender bias, discrimination, and stereotypes. This approach recognizes that men and women have different needs, experiences, and challenges in various settings. It seeks to create an inclusive environment where everyone can thrive regardless of their gender identity.

### GENIUS Gender Equality Framework (GEF)

A framework that aims to address the underlying causes of gender inequality by promoting gender equality and social inclusion. It focuses on empowering women and girls through education, health, economic empowerment, and other interventions.

### Gender Lens

A gender lens is a tool or perspective used to analyze and understand how gender dynamics influence and affect outcomes in various contexts. It helps identify and address the unique challenges faced by different genders, ensuring that policies and programs are inclusive and equitable.

### Gender Mainstreaming

Gender mainstreaming is a strategy aimed at integrating gender considerations into all aspects of policy-making, programming, and implementation processes. It seeks to ensure that gender equality is considered in all areas of work, including planning, budgeting, monitoring, and evaluation. This approach aims to systematically incorporate gender perspectives into policies, programs, and institutional processes.

#### Other terms used in conjunction with gender mainstreaming include:

- **Gender bias**: When societal beliefs, Gender stereotypes, norms, laws or gender equality (DEW) persist.
- **Gender bilinearity**: A term that refers to the idea that men and women have different needs, responsibilities, and roles in society. It highlights the importance of recognizing and addressing these differences.
- **Gender equality**: The principle that all humans should be treated equally regardless of their gender identity or expression.
- **Gender mainstreaming**: Addresses different aspects of gender inequality by incorporating a gender perspective into policies, programs, and institutional processes.
- **Gender responsive budgeting (GRB)**: A process that ensures public financial resources are allocated in ways that promote gender equality.
- **Gender transformative**: Addresses root causes of inequality (DEW) and promotes social change.

# 1 Introduction

## 1.3 Project Context

The South African National HIV Prevalence, Incidence and Behaviour Survey (SABSSM) was conducted in 2017 with the aim of assessing the impact of antiretroviral therapy (ART) on HIV incidence and prevalence. The survey also provided estimates of sexual behaviour, condom use, and HIV testing among adults aged 16-49 years. The survey included a nationally representative sample of 3,584 men and women.

# Objectives of the Report

China's sustainable development goals (SDGs) and local actions are presented to promote and strengthen health, economic, social, and environmental sustainability.

## Activities

### Activity 1: Establish a monitoring framework for the implementation of Eo-DRR into sustainable disaster preparedness programs

### Activity 2: Develop an integrated approach to support early warning systems and the development of adaptive management strategies

### Activity 3: Develop a program to strengthen local risk governance, including the establishment of early warning systems and the development of adaptive management strategies that improve the dissemination of early warnings

### Activity 4: Update the Green Book

#### Objectives for Eo-DRR include national and sub-national risk management, environmental policy development, and disaster risk reduction.

### Activity 5.1: Develop a monitoring framework to support the implementation of Eo-DRR into sustainable disaster preparedness programs

### Activity 5.2: Develop an integrated approach to support early warning systems and the development of adaptive management strategies that improve the dissemination of early warnings

### Activity 5.3: Develop a program to strengthen local risk governance, including the establishment of early warning systems and the development of adaptive management strategies that improve the dissemination of early warnings

### Activity 5.4: Update the Green Book

#### Objectives for Eo-DRR include national and sub-national risk management, environmental policy development, and disaster risk reduction.

## Objectives of the Report

China's sustainable development goals (SDGs) and local actions are presented to promote and strengthen health, economic, social, and environmental sustainability. The report aims to highlight the importance of integrating Eo-DRR into national policies and programs for achieving the SDGs. It also emphasizes the need for a comprehensive approach that includes various stakeholders such as government agencies, civil society organizations, and local communities.

The objectives of this report are:

- To provide an overview of China's sustainable development goals (SDGs) and local actions.
- To highlight the importance of integrating Eo-DRR into national policies and programs.
- To promote a comprehensive approach that includes various stakeholders such as government agencies, civil society organizations, and local communities.

The report also aims to raise awareness about the need for a holistic approach to sustainable development that addresses the interlinkages between different sectors and promotes cooperation among various stakeholders. It emphasizes the importance of integrating Eo-DRR into national policies and programs to achieve the SDGs and promote sustainable development.

and greater action plan be done for all project applications that will also supported by GC. The purpose of this analysis is to provide a comprehensive overview of the gender mainstreaming process and its impact on the project implementation and other valuable goods and also include in monitoring and evaluation system as explained in building community resilience against climate change.

This gender assessment further outlines the methodological and policy framework in South Africa and the gender-related analyses for the four projects, namely: GEF Small Grants Programme (GEF SGP), Global Environment Facility (GEF), National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and Climate Change Fund (CCF). The gender mainstreaming process is anchored on a report, highlighting the implications for women-based initiatives in climate change. The main highlighted output presents evidence, methodology, case studies and other information regarding gender that will be used to inform the project implementation.

This report is informed by a review of existing literature, secondary databases, input and perspectives of stakeholder engagement and staff from GEF SGP, GEF, NAPA and CCF projects. Most of the data was obtained from various South Africa GEF SGP, United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and GEF projects made by South Africa and the new report on gender mainstreaming in the four projects.

# Summary

A general assessment aims to compile guide information that will aid the development of an inclusive and comprehensive action plan encompassing all components and aspects of the project sites.

The findings from the general assessment were used to develop a general action plan which is also included in this report. The purpose of this plan is to provide specific and time-bound framework within which to operationalize the relevant general actions arising from the general assessment. Implementation of the plan will require ensuring that all necessary resources are available, including human resources, financial resources, technical competencies, tools, and processes to achieve the outlined results. This general action plan begins at the project level while also providing action for the intervention. However, separate general action plans will need to be prepared for individual components specific to their context and operational requirements.

# Relevant Legal Framework

## 2.1 Legal Policy and Planning Instruments for Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment in South Africa

### The Constitution of the Republic of South Africa

The Constitution of the Republic of South Africa is the supreme law of the land, binding on all persons and governing the country. Clauses 9, 10, 14, 25, 28, 30, and 31 of the Bill of Rights, which include the rights of equality, non-discrimination, and gender equality, are the foundation for non-discrimination and gender equity and in particular the promotion of women's empowerment. Other clauses also promote gender equality.

### The National Policy Framework for Women's Empowerment and Gender Equity

This policy framework was formulated by the Office on the Status of Women and is aimed at promoting gender equity and women's empowerment. It provides a framework to ensure that all policies, programmes, and activities are gender responsive and promote gender equality. It also aims to eliminate discrimination against women and enhance their participation in decision-making processes.

### Other legal frameworks for women's empowerment and gender equity

- **Promotion of Equality and Prevention of Unfair Discrimination Act (2000)**: This Act provides a framework to prevent unfair discrimination, promote equality, and provide effective remedies. It also establishes the Commission on Gender Equity.
- **Employment Equity Act (1998)**: This Act aims to achieve equity in the workplace by eliminating unfair discrimination and promoting equal opportunities for all employees regardless of race, gender, or other grounds.
- **Children's Act (Act 38 of 2005)**: This Act provides a framework for the care and protection of children. It also promotes the rights and best interests of children and requires employers to provide a safe working environment.
- **Domestic Violence Act (1998)**: This Act provides a comprehensive framework for the prevention, treatment, and rehabilitation of victims of domestic violence.

### Other relevant legislation

- **South African Schools Act (1996)**: The Act is a foundational piece of legislation in South Africa, ensuring that all learners have access to quality education. It also promotes gender equality.
- **Children's Act (Act 38 of 2005)**: This Act provides a framework for the care and protection of children. It also promotes the rights and best interests of children and requires employers to provide a safe working environment.

### Domestic Violence Act (1998)

The Act addresses the prevention, treatment, and rehabilitation of victims of domestic violence in South Africa. This legislation aims to protect women, children, and other vulnerable groups from abuse. It provides for the protection of victims, the prosecution of offenders, and the provision of support services.

### Other relevant acts

- **South African Schools Act (1996)**: The Act is a foundational piece of legislation in South Africa, ensuring that all learners have access to quality education. It also promotes gender equality.

# Criminal Law (Sexual Offences and Related Matters) Amendment Bill

The amendments to the Domestic Violence Act of 1998 were introduced through the Domestic Violence (Amendment) Bill in 2017. These amendments aim to enhance protection measures, expand the scope of offenses covered under the original DV Act, and improve the effectiveness of the Act in providing support and resources for victims of domestic abuse.

## Criminal Law (Sexual Offences and Related Matters) Amendment Bill

The Criminal Law (Sexual Offences and Related Matters) Amendment Bill is a new amendment that seeks to enhance the protection against sexual offenses. The Bill aims to strengthen existing laws, provide additional protections, and improve the criminal justice system's response to sexual offenses.

### Objectives of the Amendment Bill
- Enhance the definition of sexual offenses.
- Strengthen penalties for perpetrators.
- Improve support systems for victims.
- Encourage reporting of sexual offenses.

## Criminal Law (Sexual Offences and Related Matters) Amendment Bill (2017)

The Criminal Law (Sexual Offences and Related Matters) Amendment Bill (2017) introduces changes in the criminal justice system, evidence procedures, and penalties for sexual offenses. This includes the introduction of new offenses and aggravating factors.

### Key Changes
- Strengthen existing laws.
- Provide additional protections.
- Improve support systems for victims.
- Encourage reporting of sexual offenses.

## GCPS Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The GCPS operates under a framework of ESS that aims to promote environmental and social safeguards. These safeguard measures ensure that projects are designed, implemented, and monitored in ways that protect the environment and local communities.

### Key Components
- Environmental impact assessments.
- Social impact assessments.
- Grievance redress mechanisms.
- Community engagement.

The GCPS Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards align with the International Finance Corporation's Performance Standards and are designed to ensure that projects adhere to these standards. The GCPS safeguards include measures to protect the environment, promote social well-being, and support community development.

### Objectives
- Protect natural resources.
- Promote sustainable practices.
- Enhance community engagement.
- Ensure compliance with international standards.

## Revised Environmental and Social Policy (ES.P)

The Revised Environmental and Social Policy (ES.P) incorporates new safeguards that ensure all supported GCPS activities comply with the policy. This includes measures to protect natural resources, promote social well-being, and enhance community development.

### Key Components
- Strong and clear safeguard mechanisms.
- Integrated adherence to people and the environment.
- Strong and clear safeguard mechanisms.
- Integrated adherence to the rights of Indigenous Peoples, Sexual Aliens, and Social Groups.

2. Performance Standard 2: Labor and Working Conditions.
3. Performance Standard 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Control.

5. Performance Standard 5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement.

8. Performance Standard 8: Cultural Heritage.

## 2.1.2 SANBI Environmental and Social Standards

### SANBI Policy on Gender Mainstreaming (SANGB)

The SANGB Policy on Gender Mainstreaming is designed to affirm SANBI's policy position and commitment to contribute towards the achievement of gender equality in all its activities, programs, and projects. This policy will guide the planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of all SANBI activities.

Women represent only 40% of the global population but are responsible for 60-80% of food production. Women play a critical role in ensuring that agricultural practices are sustainable and contribute to food security. The policy is designed to ensure that women have equal access to resources, opportunities, and decision-making processes.

The policy will be implemented through the following actions:

- Highlight the need for gender equality in all SANBI activities.
- Ensure that gender issues are considered in management plans and strategies.
- Promote gender equality in all SANBI programs and projects.
- Encourage women to participate in decision-making processes.
- Provide training and capacity-building opportunities for women.
- Monitor and evaluate progress towards gender equality.

### Gender-responsive and family-friendly policies

Gender-responsive and family-friendly policies are essential to ensure that SANBI's activities are inclusive, equitable, and non-discriminatory. This policy aims to promote the well-being of all employees by providing a supportive work environment that respects diversity and promotes equal opportunities for all.

- Ensure that all policies and procedures are gender-neutral.
- Provide flexible working arrangements to support work-life balance.
- Promote a culture of respect and inclusivity.
- Monitor and evaluate progress towards gender equality.

# Importance of family maintenance

- **Highest standard of gender mainstreaming**: Ensures the need for gender mainstreaming to be integrated at the highest level in the organization, with women being given equal opportunities to shape its future.
- **Gender equality and equity**: Promotes the need for gender equality and equity, ensuring that all policies and practices are not biased against women. Encourages the inclusion of men as advocates for gender equality.
- **Non-discrimination**: Ensures non-discrimination on the basis of sex, sexual orientation, and gender identity, creating an inclusive environment where everyone is valued and respected.
- **Zero tolerance for violence**: Zero tolerance for all forms of violence against women and children. Encourages the use of other mechanisms that address violence against women and children.
- **Gender mainstreaming in SAMBO projects**: Ensures that gender mainstreaming is integrated into all externally funded projects.

# Gender Action Plan

## Objectives

- **Objective 1:** Integrate gender equality considerations and targets within all projects and programs.

### Sub-objective 1.1
Complete a Gender Assessment and Action Plans for all projects and programs.

### Sub-objective 1.2
Ensure that project and program funding proposals are required to include gender equality objectives and indicators.

## Key Activities

- **Activity 1:** Integrate gender processes, project programming, and facility application.
  - This includes completing a Gender Assessment and Action Plans for all projects and programs.
  - Ensure that project and program funding proposals are required to include gender equality objectives and indicators.

- **Activity 2:** Develop implementation and monitoring and evaluation strategies.
  - Annual activities must implement the project lead's action plan, including funding approval and follow-up on gender-related issues.
  - Monitor and evaluate progress against the gender equality objectives set out in the project/program action plans.

Overall, this framework ensures structural approaches to integrate gender goals considering SANBI’s GOPs, aligning with GCF’s Gender Policy and related standards, aiming for gender equality and sustainable climate change initiatives.

## 2.2 RELEVANT GENDER INSTITUTIONS

The key government institutions relevant to the gender objective of South Africa include:

- **Department of Women, Youth, and Persons with Disabilities (DWYPD):** Focuses on promoting and advancing gender equality.
- **Commission for Gender Equality (CGE):** Ensures that all organs of state promote and advance gender equality.

When engaging in the negotiation process to become a national initiative, key institutions involved are:

- **Department of Planning, Monitoring, and Evaluation (DPME):** Assists with monitoring and evaluation frameworks.
- **National Treasury:** Provides financial management and oversight for government programs.

The CGE has been actively engaged in promoting gender equality through various initiatives. The DPME is responsible for the monitoring and evaluation framework that ensures gender equality objectives are met across all sectors.

One other major support institution is the **Commission of Gender Equality (CGE)**, an independent national body established by Chapter 12 of the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa. It functions as a watchdog agency to ensure compliance with gender equality laws and policies, promoting equal opportunities for women and men in all spheres of life.

The CGE monitors government departments and reports on their progress towards achieving gender equality objectives, ensuring that all sectors adhere to the principles of gender equity, quality, and equity.

# Recommendations on any legislation affecting the status of women

The GEC has the authority to investigate complaints of unfair discrimination and to make recommendations to government, private sector and other employers, providing input to its advocacy efforts.

In terms of the Gender Equity Commission (GEC) Act, the GEC operates an anonymous hotline with the NWPD. It focuses on advising for the enforcement of laws and promoting fairer conditions in the workplace. The primary goal is to empower that those denied justice, whether the employer or employee, to have their grievances addressed. The GEC also provides a platform for employers to report unfair discrimination, harassment, sexual harassment, and other forms of unfair treatment.

The GEC's mandate includes investigating complaints from employees and employers, providing mediation services, and offering guidance on workplace policies. The GEC is committed to addressing gender-related issues. The GEC operates under the Ministry of Women, Youth and Persons with Disabilities, providing updates on activities, findings, and recommendations. Funds for the GEC are primarily sourced from government grants, although additional funding comes from private sector partnerships.

## Relevant Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs)

NGOs play a crucial role in advocating issues affecting women's rights. These organizations work closely with the government to promote gender equality and empower women. They also provide support for victims of discrimination and violence, promoting fair treatment and equal opportunities.

NGOs contribute significantly to addressing gender-related issues by providing more visible and effective solutions. Some notable NGOs include:

- **Gender Links**: Gender Links is a regional NGO that promotes gender equality and women's empowerment in Southern Africa. It works with various stakeholders to promote gender equality and women's rights.
- **Women and Men's Action for Change (WMAC)**: WMAC is an organization that focuses on promoting gender equality and empowering women and girls. They work closely with communities to address issues of gender-based violence and discrimination.

# Women People to People (P2P)

# Mandela Mandela Aids Project for Combating Stigma

- The Mandela AIDS Project for Combating Stigma operates in the Mhlongwa region in collaboration with the Society of Family Health South Africa.

# Sexual Reproductive Health Rights (SRHR)

- Sexual Reproductive Health Rights is an organisation that works with women, including SRHR activists, and study-based prevention programmes. They work on the same issues in South Africa, with a focus on providing SRHR services to young people.

- The Human Syndemic project aims to address the health needs of women and girls who are highly affected by the COVID-19 pandemic. However, some have called for more research to be conducted on the impact of the syndemic on women's health.

# RELEVANT INTERNATIONAL CONVENTIONS

One and other national legal instruments, South Africa is a signatory to several international conventions and protocols. The country has also ratified a host of its ILO International Labour Organisation (ILO) Conventions. These 27 conventions ratified by South Africa, are in force. Signing a treaty represents the intention to abide by the treaty law by a state.

The key principles regarding human rights are entrenched in the following international instruments:

1. **Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination** (ICERD), signed 9 August 1965, entered into force 4 January 1969.
2. **International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights**, with The International Declaration of Human Rights is a foundational document that provides for the realisation of economic, social and cultural rights in a progressive manner.
3. **Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women** (CEDAW), signed 2 August 1980, entered into force 3 September 1981.
4. **Convention on the Rights of the Child**, signed 16 September 1990, entered into force 2 September 1990.
5. **Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment** (CAT), signed 18 October 1994, entered into force 26 June 1987.
6. **Declaration on the Protection and Integration of Human Rights**, with The Universal Declaration of Human Rights is a foundational document that provides for the realisation of human rights in a progressive manner.
7. **Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities** (CRPD), signed 30 March 2015, entered into force 3 May 2008.

The following conventions are also relevant to South Africa:

- The **African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights**, adopted by the Organisation of African Unity in 1981.
- The **Convention on the Rights of the Child** (CRC), signed 26 June 1990, entered into force 2 September 1990.

The ILO conventions include:

- **Forced Labour Convention, 1930 (No. 29)**.
- **Abolition of Forced Labour Convention, 1957 (No. 105)**.
- **Discrimination (Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 (No. 111)**.

South Africa has also ratified the following ILO conventions:

- **Equal Remuneration Convention, 1951 (No. 100)**.
- **Abolition of Forced Labour Convention, 1957 (No. 105)**.
- **Discrimination (Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 (No. 111)**.

The ILO conventions are also relevant to South Africa:

- **Forced Labour Convention, 1930 (No. 29)**.
- **Abolition of Forced Labour Convention, 1957 (No. 105)**.
- **Discrimination (Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 (No. 111)**.

# 2.3.1 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

South Africa is committed to achieving SDG number 5: The gender equality review showed significant progress in reducing gender inequalities and promoting greater women's empowerment, as well as the implementation of gender training and policies.

South Africa has extended inclusive education and legislation to promote gender equality, including interventions that enhance women's ability to participate fully in society. These include the introduction of gender-responsive budgeting, gender mainstreaming, and gender-responsive planning, which are aimed at addressing gender inequalities and promoting development outcomes.

A new report from Stats SA (Stats) indicates progress in maternal health, specifically a decrease in the Maternal Mortality Ratio in Family Planning in 2019. The MMR decreased by approximately 6% between 2015 and 2018. This is an improvement compared to previous years.

The report also highlights the importance of family planning and the implementation of the National Health Insurance (NHI) system, which has been endorsed by the World Health Organization (WHO). The NHI aims to provide universal health coverage for all South Africans, regardless of their socio-economic status. This includes the provision of primary healthcare services.

The report also discusses the accessibility of reproductive health services during the COVID-19 pandemic and the importance of maintaining these services despite the challenges posed by the pandemic.

Human trafficking remains a significant concern, particularly in relation to women and children. The government has implemented various measures aimed at addressing this issue, including legislation, awareness campaigns, and support for victims.

South Africa has established various Gender Equality Structures, Monitoring, Evaluation, and Auditing Frameworks, the National Strategic Plan on Gender-Based Violence, and the Integrated Framework to address gender-based violence and promote gender equality and women's empowerment.

Despite these efforts, there are still significant challenges that need to be addressed. These include the high levels of gender-based violence, the lack of access to justice for victims, and the persistence of traditional practices that perpetuate gender inequality.

The report also highlights the importance of education in promoting gender equality, particularly in the poorest sectors. Women in the country often have lower levels of education than men.

The government is committed to addressing these challenges and working towards achieving the SDGs by 2030.

# Demographic and Women Empowerment Indicators

## National Gender Inequality

### Situation of Women in the Country

South Africa's population consists of 50.8% females according to the last Census by Stats SA. Out of this approximately 29 million people:

- 17 million are female adults, the age of group includes the highest proportion of the total.
- 64% of women are in the economically active age group (15-64 years).
- 30% of women are aged between 18 and 24 years.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) for South Africa was 0.479 in 2019, which is higher than the global average of 0.415.

A significant gender wage gap exists between men and women. The average monthly income for a man is R16,381 while that for a woman is R11,938. This gender pay gap results in lower female participation rates in the labour market compared to males. Women are also more likely to be employed in low-paying jobs.

Gender-based violence (GBV) remains a significant issue in South Africa. According to Stats SA, 1 in 5 women have experienced GBV. The high incidence of GBV is linked to social and economic factors such as poverty, unemployment, and alcohol abuse.

Nearly 29% of households are headed by females, indicating a shift from the traditional family structure where men were the primary breadwinners. This change has been attributed to various factors including HIV/AIDS, which has resulted in higher mortality rates among males.

Statistics show that over 50% of children have both parents living together. Disability is more prevalent among women (8%) than men (6%). The majority of people with disabilities are unemployed and live below the poverty line. The unemployment rate for people with disabilities is significantly higher compared to those without disabilities.

### Gender Indicies and Ratios

South Africa has made significant progress in recent years towards gender equality, as reflected in its Constitution and various laws that promote equal rights for men and women. However, despite these efforts, disparities still exist between genders in terms of employment, education, and other areas.

The Global Gender Gap Index provides a measure that shows progress towards gender parity, including gender gaps across economic, political, health and educational spheres. It is designed to create a metric that would allow comparison in any country of the world.

South Africa scored 0.69 on the index, placing it at 18th out of 142 countries. The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) policy with a 0.73 score shows that South Africa has closed 73% of its gender gap. The GGGI measures gender equality in four areas: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment.

### 3.3 Financial Resources, Poverty and Employment

The system needs to free individuals to seek women's health participation progress in the realm of development and economic empowerment. It also ensures participation with various efforts, including legal rights, communication works, and self-reliance. Economic empowerment leads to a positive woman/girl poverty reduction or improvement in their living standards. The government has implemented several programs to improve women's financial status. Studies highlight the great disparity in access to resources, especially for rural and urban households. However, women's participation in the labor market is still limited by cultural norms and gender roles.

#### Participation in Labor Market

In 2014, a participation rate among participants was observed for males and females who lived with 5 or more children at 94.8% and 93.6%, respectively. Data from the Ministry of Manpower and Transmigration (2014) showed that women's participation in the labor market is still low, especially in rural areas. However, there are efforts being made to improve this situation.

A high percentage of households living with children are found among poor households, ranging from 5% for those with 3 or more children. On the other hand, many children live in households headed by women who work as domestic workers and are not married. The number of children living with single mothers is increasing. This indicates that the number of children living with single mothers is increasing.

#### Unique Pay and Unpaid Work

The year 2014 and household surveys once more confirmed that females spend a significant amount of their time on unpaid work, including child care and cooking, while males are more involved in collecting fuel and water around the local groups. In addition to this, women also spend more time working with livestock and gardening (GDP) studies highlight the importance of women's participation in the economy. The GDP studies point out that women are involved in various activities, including farming, fishing, and forestry, and the value of goods from subsistence agriculture. The contribution of females to the household income is significant.

# Females were more likely to receive grants than their male counterparts irrespective of where they studied

Females were more likely to receive grants than their male counterparts irrespective of where they studied. This is consistent with previous studies that have shown females are more likely to be employed than males and less likely to be unemployed (that is, not seeking work). Women are also more likely to be in part-time employment (that is, working fewer hours per week) than men. The gender gap in employment rates is larger among older age groups with the largest gaps observed for those aged 50-64 years. This is consistent with previous studies that have shown women are less likely to be employed than men and more likely to be unemployed (that is, not seeking work). Women are also more likely to be in part-time employment (that is, working fewer hours per week) than men. The gender gap in employment rates is larger among older age groups with the largest gaps observed for those aged 50-64 years.

## Education Attainment and Occupation Choices

There is a relationship between education attainment and occupation choices across both genders. In terms of educational attainment, there was a very small proportion of females (1%) who had no qualifications compared to males (3%). A higher proportion of females (28%) had a degree or equivalent qualification compared to males (24%). Females were more likely to be in professional occupations than males, although the gap is smaller for younger groups (18-29 years) compared to older age groups.

### Women occupying management positions account for 3.8% compared to 6.8% for men

| Category                | Male   | Female |
|-------------------------|--------|--------|
| Current employment     | 50%    | 45%    |
| Professional occupations| 28%    | 24%    |
| Management positions   | 3.8%   | 6.8%   |

![Figure 1.1: Representation of females in some employment (2019)](image_url)

In terms of employment, the representation of females in the jobs that they studied was highest for those who studied medicine and health (75%) compared to other fields. This is consistent with previous studies that have shown women are more likely to be employed than men and more likely to be unemployed (that is, not seeking work). Women are also more likely to be in part-time employment (that is, working fewer hours per week) than men. The gender gap in employment rates is larger among older age groups with the largest gaps observed for those aged 50-64 years.

### 3.1.4 Access to Water, Land and Energy

Gender equality is important for access to water for both productive and household use and sanitation. Productive uses of water include irrigation, livestock watering, and small-scale aquaculture. Women are more likely to be involved in these activities than men. In many countries, women did not have paid work outside the home.

# Newer, though questionable, efforts to save impoverished Etn. Oromo/Shanashakle

In Ethiopia, impoverished Etn. Oromo/Shanashakle still suffer from ethnic water/household, sanitation, and hygiene problems. The Ethiopian government has recently launched a five-year plan for 62% of all households with 152,000 household toilets, latrines, and 4.63 million household water points. But the United Nations estimates that 80% of Ethiopia's rural population lacks access to safe drinking water, sanitation, and hygiene.
