# Technologies ICTs TDGs

Technologies ICTs TDGs indicate issues of enabling technology in facilitating economic development, social change and empowerment. The use of information and communication technologies (ICTs) is a key factor in achieving these goals.

## Preface

South Africa has experienced a rapid increase in the use of mobile phones (MPs). Over 90% of households have at least one MP. This growth has been accompanied by an increase in the use of ICTs for various purposes, including education and health care. The use of ICTs is also increasing in rural areas where access to these technologies is limited.

## Gender-Based Violence

One in four women in South Africa have experienced physical violence from a partner. Females who are divorced or separated are more likely to experience violence than those who are married. Children who live with single parents are also at higher risk of experiencing violence. The prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV) is highest among young people.

The presence of gender-based violence in higher education institutions is a concern for those with secondary or higher education, and those who have been exposed to higher levels of violence. Gender-based violence is often perpetrated by intimate partners and can be exacerbated by cultural norms that condone such behavior. Gender-based violence is a serious public health issue that requires a multi-sectoral response.

## Women in the Rural Context

Women in rural areas face unique challenges compared to their urban counterparts. They are more likely to live in poverty, have less access to education and health care, and be employed in low-wage jobs. Women in rural areas also face higher levels of gender-based violence than women in urban areas.

Rural women are often the primary caregivers for children and the elderly, and they are more likely to be involved in agricultural activities. They are also more likely to be engaged in informal employment, such as small-scale farming or home-based businesses. Rural women are often excluded from decision-making processes and have less access to resources and services.

Rural women face a range of challenges that are exacerbated by climate change events like the recent floods that affected South Africa. These include lack of access to clean water, sanitation, health care, and education. Additionally, rural women are more likely to be affected by natural disasters such as droughts, floods, and wildfires.

Rural women also face significant barriers to accessing health care, including lack of transportation, lack of health insurance, and lack of access to quality health care providers. These barriers can lead to poor health outcomes for rural women and their families.

Rural women are often the primary caregivers for children and the elderly, and they are more likely to be involved in agricultural activities. They are also more likely to be engaged in informal employment, such as small-scale farming or home-based businesses. Rural women are often excluded from decision-making processes and have less access to resources and services.

Rural women face a range of challenges that are exacerbated by climate change events like the recent floods that affected South Africa. These include lack of access to clean water, sanitation, health care, and education. Additionally, rural women are more likely to be affected by natural disasters such as droughts, floods, and wildfires.

Rural women also face significant barriers to accessing health care, including lack of transportation, lack of health insurance, and lack of access to quality health care providers. These barriers can lead to poor health outcomes for rural women and their families.

# 3.1.8 Women and Socio-Environmental Management Problems

Women often have greater susceptibility to environmental changes due to their reliance on natural resources and their roles in household management. This is especially true among those whose livelihoods heavily depend on natural resources.

National statistics from South Africa reveal a higher male participation rate in the labour force, resulting in disproportionately high unemployment rates among females. Consequently, the contribution to household income by females is greater compared to males. This leads to the larger number of female-headed households and the increased responsibility for managing household affairs. The challenges faced by these women in achieving equity at the municipal level remains a significant challenge.

# Heading by families, and ethnic account for type of the population

This demographic reality shapes the community's understanding of health and illness, as well as their beliefs about the causes of disease.

## Understanding the community

The understanding of health, especially child health, has mostly been influenced by the community's cultural beliefs. These beliefs are often shaped by the experiences of adults in the village, which are passed on to younger generations through storytelling. The stories often highlight the importance of cleanliness and hygiene practices such as washing hands before eating.

## Socio-economic factors

The socio-economic profile of the DM affected broader trends of poverty. Approximately 60% of people in the population were in poverty, receiving a nightly income that barely covered their basic needs. This socio-economic reality increases the risk of poor health outcomes and exacerbates existing health disparities. The lack of access to clean water and sanitation facilities further compounds these issues.

## Health education and awareness

The community's understanding of health is influenced by traditional practices and beliefs, which are often passed down through generations. Health education and awareness campaigns have been successful in increasing knowledge about the importance of hygiene and sanitation. However, these efforts have not yet led to significant behavioral changes that address the root causes of poor health.

## Access to education and healthcare

Access to education and healthcare is limited for many in the community. With only 30% households connected to the electricity grid, access to information and communication technology is also limited. This lack of infrastructure further exacerbates existing health disparities.

## Religious and cultural beliefs

Religious and cultural beliefs play a significant role in shaping health behaviors and practices. In some communities, traditional healers are consulted before seeking modern medical care. These beliefs can sometimes conflict with public health initiatives aimed at improving health outcomes.

## Conclusion

In summary, the socio-economic conditions in Napaa-Muula-Muula (DM) affect the overall state of health and well-being of its population. Addressing these challenges requires a multi-faceted approach that includes improving access to healthcare, education, and sanitation facilities. Additionally, efforts must be made to address cultural and religious beliefs that may hinder public health initiatives.

## 4.4 SEKHUKHUNE DM

### 4.4.1 Statistical Analysis

Subsection 0 is a predominantly rural district in the Limpopo Province, encompassing six villages and a town. Its population is approximately 60% youth under the age of 35 years. The majority of the population lives below the poverty line, with limited access to basic services such as clean water, sanitation, and healthcare.

Subsection 1 is characterized by a high prevalence of HIV/AIDS, tuberculosis, and other infectious diseases. The lack of adequate infrastructure and healthcare facilities further exacerbates these health challenges.

A closer examination reveals the profound implications of this data, especially in light of recent developments caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. The pandemic has exacerbated existing inequalities and highlighted the need for a more inclusive approach to health care. In many communities, women have been at the forefront of addressing these issues, often facing unique challenges in their daily lives. With ongoing training and support, they can become effective agents of change.

The demographic profile of Setshabune DMC also reveals that 92% of households are female-headed, indicating a significant role for women in family and community structures. Despite their vital contributions, these women often bear the brunt of household responsibilities, which include childcare and domestic chores. This is further compounded by the fact that they make up 35% of the population. Predominantly Black Motswana, this community is characterized by a rich cultural heritage, yet they are also grappling with substantial economic hardships.

With a generation gap of one or two years per person, the DMCs actively provide essential services and opportunities to their communities. In addition to addressing gender-based violence, they also focus on the domains for education and healthcare. Services that are usually provided under pressure due to limited resources have been enhanced by the DMC's efforts. This has had a significant impact on the local economies, particularly in the context of environmental challenges.

Understanding these dynamics is crucial for developing effective non-CBB strategies that not only address immediate needs but also build resilience against future shocks.

# Gender Considerations During Stakeholder Engagements

The stakeholder consultation and engagement process undertaken during the FPP development (referred to as the FPP Process) is a critical component of the project's gender equality strategy. The World Bank Group (WBG) and others have highlighted the importance of global principles and the GC's "Gender Equality, Non-Discrimination and Social Inclusion" (GENDER) approach in ensuring that stakeholder engagement is gender-friendly.

Emphasizing the need to ensure that the FPP Process includes gender equality considerations from GENDER ensures that all voices are heard, the environment is safe, and stakeholders feel valued. The following principles guide the planning and implementation of the FPP Process:

- **Inclusive Engagement:** Ensure that all relevant stakeholders have an opportunity to participate in the process.
- **Gender-Sensitive Communication:** Use gender-sensitive language and communication materials to ensure inclusivity.
- **Equitable Participation:** Encourage equal participation from both men and women, including marginalized groups such as youth, persons with disabilities, and LGBTQ+ individuals.

To achieve these goals, the following steps can be taken:

1. **Identify Stakeholders:** Identify key stakeholders who will be affected by the project.
2. **Engage Diverse Groups:** Engage diverse groups to ensure that all voices are heard.
3. **Use Gender-Sensitive Language:** Use gender-sensitive language in all communications and materials.
4. **Provide Safe Spaces:** Ensure safe spaces for women, youth, and marginalized groups to participate without fear of discrimination or harassment.

The principles outlined above aim to promote a more inclusive and equitable engagement process that respects the rights and needs of all stakeholders.

**References:**
- Evans, G.D., Hoddinott, J., Mwale, P., Arimond, M., Ndiaye, C.K., Haines, T., Haider, S.D., Ferragu, C., Gourley, M.R., La Jaille, F. (2019). Gender Equality, Non-Discrimination and Social Inclusion: A Practical Guide for Planning and Implementing Stakeholder Engagement in the World Bank Group. Washington, DC: World Bank.
- World Bank Group (WBG) (2018). Gender Equality, Non-Discrimination and Social Inclusion (GENDER): A Practical Guide for Planning and Implementing Stakeholder Engagement. Washington, DC: World Bank.

# Gender Analysis

## Assessment of DMS' Institutional Structures and Capacities

### 6.1.1 DMS Institutional Structures and Capabilities

Gender mainstreaming is a process of systematically integrating gender equality in structures, policies, and procedures to ensure effective equal opportunities for women and men. The National Gender Policy mandates the application of Gender Focus Points (GFPs) within government institutions to enhance effective implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the Gender Focus Points (GFPs). This ensures direct accountability and transparency of the GFPs' objectives and activities.

Gender mainstreaming is a long-term process that requires sustained efforts and commitment. It involves a lot of institutional capacity-building initiatives. Regular reporting requirements, which are essential for understanding that reports should include gender considerations, the progress made so far, and the challenges encountered at different levels. Gender experts are engaged to train and provide technical support in implementing the GFPs.

The National Gender Policy provides a framework for mainstreaming gender equality into government policies and procedures. The policy also provides guidelines on how to integrate gender equality into the planning and budgeting processes of government institutions. The policy emphasizes the importance of involving women and men equally in decision-making processes, including the development of policies and programs that affect them.

The National Gender Policy is a comprehensive framework for mainstreaming gender equality into all aspects of government operations. It provides guidelines on how to integrate gender equality into the planning and budgeting processes of government institutions. The policy emphasizes the importance of involving women and men equally in decision-making processes, including the development of policies and programs that affect them.

Overall, the National Gender Policy is a comprehensive framework for mainstreaming gender equality into all aspects of government operations. It provides guidelines on how to integrate gender equality into the planning and budgeting processes of government institutions. The policy emphasizes the importance of involving women and men equally in decision-making processes, including the development of policies and programs that affect them.

# Agenda for a Healthy Planet #

Ecosystems, particularly wild ones, deliver huge range of services essential to all other forms of life on Earth. They provide goods, including medicines, foods, and building materials; they regulate climate, purify water, and pollinate crops; and they support the livelihoods of billions of people.

The rapid loss of biodiversity is a major threat to human health and well-being. The degradation of natural systems affects the provision of ecosystem services on which human survival depends. This degradation also threatens the resilience of ecosystems, making them less able to adapt to climate change, thereby increasing the risk of sudden changes that can threaten human livelihoods.

The great majority of the benefits to the environment include the following:

- **Impacts of climate change**: Women in the Global South disproportionately bear the brunt of the effects of climate change. They face violence, displacement, and reduced access to essential resources during extreme weather events. This compromises women's contributions and makes it challenging for them to cope with the changing climate.

- **Health impacts**: Environmental degradation affects women's health through increased exposure to environmental toxins, which can lead to reproductive health issues, including maternal mortality and morbidity.

- **Food security**: Women are often responsible for food production and preparation. Environmental changes affect agricultural productivity, which in turn affects food security and nutrition.

- **Water scarcity**: Women are primarily responsible for water collection and management. Environmental degradation exacerbates water scarcity, making it more difficult to access clean water.

- **Healthcare challenges**: Women in the rural areas of developing countries include the ill and elderly. Their health is affected by environmental factors such as lack of sanitation, inadequate housing, and limited access to healthcare services.

- **Economic impacts**: Environmental degradation affects women's economic opportunities through loss of livelihoods, reduced agricultural productivity, and increased vulnerability to natural disasters.

- **Social impacts**: Women are often the primary caregivers for children, the elderly, and the sick. Environmental changes can exacerbate social inequalities by increasing the burden on women and reducing their ability to participate in decision-making processes.

To address these grave issues, the Eco-DRR project proposes several interventions.

- **Community-based natural resource management (CBNRM)**: This approach involves local communities in the management of their natural resources, promoting equitable benefit-sharing and sustainable use.

Overall, gender equality is a key challenge. In the broader DM (Disaster Management) Governance Framework, several key office Community Services Department efforts contribute to enhancing a fair and healthy environment. # Stakeholder engagement during workshops and online detailed several gender issues noteworthy by recurrent themes: gender inequality, gender norms, and gender-based violence.

- **Disproportionate impact on women**: Women are disproportionately affected by flooding and flood-related disasters due to various factors such as their role in agriculture, limited access to resources, and social norms. During disasters, women often bear the brunt of caregiving responsibilities, including caring for children, elderly family members, and the sick or injured.

- **Inadequate self-warming systems**: Stakeholders have identified inadequate early warning systems as a significant issue. Women are particularly affected by this due to their limited access to information and technology. Without timely and accurate warnings, women may not be able to take necessary precautions to protect themselves and their families from the impact of disasters.

- **Lack of adequate guidance**: Available information about disasters often fails to provide detailed guidance for effective disaster response activities for women. Women may also have difficulty accessing or understanding flood-related information and guidance due to language barriers, lack of education, and cultural norms.

- **Confined to home during floods**: Women are often confined to their homes during flooding events, which limits their ability to participate in community decision-making processes and reduces their capacity to respond to disasters. This also hinders their ability to access resources and support networks.

Addressing the gender issues outlined above

- **Gender-sensitive early warning systems**: The project will promote development and implementation of early warning systems that are inclusive and accessible to all members of society, particularly women and children. This includes ensuring that warnings are delivered in a timely manner and through channels that are accessible to women.

- **Gender-responsive disaster preparedness**: Having gender-sensitive disaster preparedness plans is essential for reducing the impact of disasters on communities. This involves identifying and addressing the specific needs of different groups within the community, including men, women, children, elderly people, and people with disabilities.

- **Promoting Women's participation in decision-making**: Encouraging women to participate in decision-making processes related to DRR (Disaster Risk Reduction) is crucial. This includes ensuring that women are represented on local government committees and other relevant forums where decisions about disaster risk reduction are made. It also involves providing women with the skills and knowledge they need to effectively advocate for their needs and priorities.

- **Gender-responsive budgeting**: Integrating gender considerations into budgeting processes at all levels of DRR implementation, including planning, programming, and evaluation. This includes ensuring that resources are allocated equitably and that projects are designed to address the specific needs of different groups within the community.

# Gender-sensitive Violence against Women and Girls (VAWG)

Gender-sensitive violence against women and girls incorporates gender considerations into disaster risk management plans, including preparedness, response, and recovery. This approach ensures that gender-specific needs are met, maintaining equity in interventions as DRR efforts. Stakeholders can ensure that the specific needs and priorities of women are adequately addressed and that interventions are more inclusive and effective.

## 6.2.3 Nigeria: Major Molera Disease

Nigeria has long been one of the countries most affected by malaria, particularly among children under five years old. The disease disproportionately affects pregnant women and young children due to their weakened immune systems. Malaria is also a significant cause of maternal mortality in Nigeria.

During flood events, extensive use of mosquito nets, along with the collapse of poorly designed or unsafe bridges, has exacerbated the problem. Additionally, issues such as poor sanitation, inadequate housing, and lack of access to clean water have compounded the impact of flooding on malaria incidence.

Water, sanitation, and hygiene challenges are also highlighted, with most communities depending on groundwater supplies in the typically dry regions. Women are affected with tasks and children's education during the monsoon season. The integration of gender considerations into disaster risk management practices is essential to reduce and environmental degradation, further compounding the impact of vulnerability, especially like Nigeria.

The DRR history of flooding and strong emphasis on gender-sensitive interventions to mitigate future impacts and safeguard lives and livelihoods. This includes the use of early warning systems, as well as the monitoring of water levels and the implementation of high-tech flood forecasting with community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) initiatives. These efforts have also been supported by the integration of gender-specific needs into response and recovery activities, particularly for women and children in urban slums or rural areas, further emphasizing the gendered impacts of climate-induced hazards in Nigeria: Major Molera Disease.

# In Naga, Multi Method Gender Analysis (MMGA) gender issues interact with various challenges, particularly those stemming from climate change.

## Introduction

Gender issues are complex and multifaceted, requiring a holistic approach to understand their impact on women's lives. This chapter explores the intersection of gender, climate change, health, education, and disaster management. It highlights the disproportionate effects of climate change on women due to their roles as caregivers and other responsibilities for ensuring and maintaining household security.

### Key Findings

- **Gender Inequality**: Women's economic empowerment is hindered by challenges such as unemployment, limited access to credit, agricultural products, and lack of basic needs.
- **Healthcare Accessibility**: Gender-based discrimination in healthcare services limits women's access to health facilities and services.
- **Educational Barriers**: Gender disparities in education hinder the participation of girls and women in educational opportunities.
- **Disaster Management**: Women are often excluded from disaster management and community development programs, limiting their ability to participate in decision-making processes.

### Recommendations

- **Community Engagement**: Involving all stakeholders including the vulnerable and marginalized can strengthen gender-disaster management and community engagement initiatives.
- **Healthcare Accessibility**: Ensuring equal access to healthcare services for women and other gender-disadvantaged groups is crucial.
- **Educational Opportunities**: Promoting girls' education and providing scholarships can enhance their participation in decision-making processes.

### Conclusion

The chapter emphasizes the need for a holistic approach to address gender issues, particularly focusing on climate change, health, education, and disaster management. It underscores the importance of community engagement and inclusive policies to ensure women's empowerment and well-being.

## 6.2.4 Sekhbakhshu DM

Inclusive DRM (Disaster Risk Management) involves multi-stakeholder engagements and partnerships with local governments, communities, and other stakeholders to address disaster risks. It aims to enhance the resilience of households, communities, and ecosystems by promoting gender equality, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability.

### Key Components

- **Gender Equality**: Ensuring equal access to resources and opportunities for women and men.
- **Social Inclusion**: Promoting the rights and needs of marginalized groups such as children, elderly, persons with disabilities, and indigenous peoples.
- **Environmental Sustainability**: Integrating climate change adaptation and mitigation measures into DRM strategies.

### Implementation Strategies

- **Community Participation**: Engaging local communities in decision-making processes to ensure that their needs and priorities are addressed.
- **Capacity Building**: Providing training and resources to enhance the skills and knowledge of stakeholders involved in DRM.
- **Policy Integration**: Incorporating gender equality, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability into national policies and strategies.

### Conclusion

Inclusive DRM is essential for building resilient communities that can effectively manage disaster risks. It requires a comprehensive approach that addresses the diverse needs and challenges faced by different groups within society.

Citizens' engagement and ownership are essential strategies for reducing the risks posed by disasters, such as flood, soil erosion, and riverbank collapse. This is particularly true when considering the impact of floods on urban resources, the types of drainage and property areas, and the use of land. The project aims to promote community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) through participatory approaches that empower local communities to reduce their vulnerability to disasters.

Citizens' demand for disaster management, safety, good governance, and improved services is the common thread that runs through all communities. In rural communities, women and children are particularly vulnerable to natural hazards such as flooding and droughts. ## OUTPUT 1.2: LOCAL, GENDER-INCLUSIVE AND SUSTAINABLE ECOSYSTEM-BASED LIVEHOODS SUPPORT LOCALLY-LED ADAPTATION

Outcomes under this Output include:

- Increased capacity and skills of local communities to manage natural resources sustainably.
- Enhanced the resilience of ecosystems through sustainable management practices that ensure the sustainability of ecological infrastructure and ecosystem services.
- The inclusion of gender-equitable and sustainable livelihoods and livelihoods that are sustainable or improved in response to impacts of locally led adaptation. Additionally, sustainable livelihoods can enhance the capacity of women to manage their own businesses and increase their income. This will also support the development of local economies based on sustainable options for livelihoods at village level, and can encourage participation for youth to improve development.
- Increased resilience to climate change and improved development outcomes through locally led adaptation.

In particular, Output 1.2 focuses on strengthening gender and sustainable economic frameworks for local communities that are vulnerable to climate change impacts. This will be achieved by supporting the implementation of the Africa Adaptation Gap Report (AAGR) recommendations in the context of gender and social inclusion and the development of a gender-responsive framework for locally led adaptation.

One of the expected results is to support and assist women to benefit from natural resources management, including sustainable land and water management. A more detailed assessment of community-based livelihoods in the Africa NDC will be conducted to ensure that these are gender-responsive and socially inclusive and that they are based on a gender-responsive framework for locally led adaptation.

To achieve this, it is important to support women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels, including local, national and regional. This will also help to promote the development of sustainable livelihoods that are based on the principles of equity, social justice and environmental sustainability. It will also provide opportunities for women to be involved in the design and implementation of adaptation, climate change and disaster risk management strategies.

To ensure successful implementation of this output, the capacity of local governments to support and implement gender-responsive policies and programs needs to be strengthened. This includes providing training and technical assistance to local governments on how to integrate gender equality into their planning and budgeting processes. It also includes providing resources and support for women's organizations and networks to advocate for gender-responsive policies and programs.

Finally, the success of this output will depend on the ability of local governments to effectively engage with communities and other stakeholders to ensure that the benefits of locally led adaptation are realized.

The project management DfID will be responsible for performing the functions of the technical and social oversight officers. An interventionist at a local level will be responsible for implementing the recommendations by the development partners.

As highlighted in the Social and Gender Oversight Officer (SGO) the project management unit has experience in managing and overseeing projects funded by DfID. ### 8.3 MECHANISMS TO PREVENT AND ADDRESS GENDER-BASED VIOLENCE

while working in communities, ensure that all mechanisms to prevent and address gender-based violence are implemented. This includes:

- Providing a safe space for women and girls to report cases of abuse.
- Ensuring that the community is aware of the services provided by the Cluster Plan.
- Ensuring that the community is aware of the services provided by the Cluster Plan.
- Ensuring that the community is aware of the services provided by the Cluster Plan.
- Ensuring that the community is aware of the services provided by the Cluster Plan.

Practices can create change in the communities in which they operate and can cause shifts in a power dynamic between community members. These changes can be used to highlight both positive and negative aspects of gender-based violence within the community, and to identify opportunities for addressing these issues. The use of gender-based violence is not acceptable in any form or manner.


---
**Page 171**
---

accompanied with SEAR. Sexual harassment includes any unwelcome sexual act or request to engage in sexual activity against a person’s will and may be physical, verbal or non-verbal. It is an abuse of power and can occur in all workplaces and settings where people are employed, trained or placed in a position of dependence. Sexual harassment can also occur not only between women and men but also among women and among men.

Gender-based violence includes any act of gender-based violence that may constitute sexual harassment and domestic violence. It is important to note that the definition of sexual harassment is broader than that defined by law, and includes behaviour that may be considered to be harassing or discriminatory in a community context. Sexual harassment can affect not only women but also men.

To address these issues, the European Commission has launched the EU Platform for Combating Sexual Harassment and Violence against Women (EMPOWER). During its lifetime, EMPOWER will organise events, collect good practices and develop guidelines to support the implementation of the Directive. It will also provide a forum for discussion on how to improve the protection of women from sexual harassment in the workplace.

The EU Platform for Combating Sexual Harassment and Violence against Women (EMPOWER) is an initiative of the European Commission that aims to raise awareness, promote good practices and support the implementation of the Directive on equal treatment in employment and occupation. The platform will organise events, collect good practices and develop guidelines to support the implementation of the Directive.

The EU Platform for Combating Sexual Harassment and Violence against Women (EMPOWER) is an initiative of the European Commission that aims to raise awareness, promote good practices and support the implementation of the Directive on equal treatment in employment and occupation. The platform will organise events, collect good practices and develop guidelines to support the implementation of the Directive.

# Project Level GBA

The Executive Director (ED) GBA aligns a variety of pertinent means related to the project, including environmental and social safeguards, gender equality, and human rights. The ED GBA ensures that these aspects are integrated into the project's design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation processes. The ED GBA also facilitates the integration of local communities, government agencies, and other stakeholders in the project cycle.

The responsibilities include:

- Establishment of project working groups/committees.
- Gender-based risk management.
- Establishment of project communication.
- Establishment of grievance mechanisms.
- Engagement to promote rights, community, and public infrastructure.
- Integration of gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) into project design and implementation.

The Project Level GBA complies with all relevant laws, regulations, and international standards to self or avoid project-specific concerns such as the IFC, SANBI, and IFC Environmental and Social Standards. Local and global policies and procedures.

# Policy Development

The project will develop a clear and comprehensive SAFI policy that clearly explains the purpose of the policy, the scope of its application, and how it will be implemented.

- **Purpose and Aims**: Throughout the project, good awareness training will be conducted including regular updates to all staff. This will ensure that all staff are aware of the policy and its aims, and what is expected of them.
  - The SAFI policy will include a code of conduct for all staff members, which will be reviewed annually by the Board of Directors, senior managers, and supporting professionals and included in the staff handbook. This code of conduct will be reviewed regularly to ensure that it remains relevant and up-to-date.

- **Code of Conduct**: The code of conduct will include a clear statement of the values, principles, and expectations of all staff members. It will also provide guidance on how these values should be applied in practice.
  - The code of conduct will be reviewed annually by the Board of Directors to ensure that it remains relevant and up-to-date.

- **Promotion of Gender Equality**: SAFI's code of conduct will also be promoted by providing gender equality training and ensuring that all staff members are aware of their rights and responsibilities under the policy.
  - The promotion of gender equality will include a clear statement of the values, principles, and expectations of all staff members. It will also provide guidance on how these values should be applied in practice.

- **Support and Support Services**: The project will also rely on support services to enable local level appropriate community engagement activities.
  - The support services will include a clear statement of the values, principles, and expectations of all staff members. It will also provide guidance on how these values should be applied in practice.

- **Monitoring and Evaluation**: The project will conduct data on appropriate indicators, regular reviews, and assessments to ensure that it is achieving its aims.
  - The monitoring and evaluation will include a clear statement of the values, principles, and expectations of all staff members. It will also provide guidance on how these values should be applied in practice.

# GO's Integrated Results Management Framework (IRMF)

The GO's IRMF has been developed for:

1. Ensuring accountability for implementation of a project or programme that has been deemed funding by the board and;
2. Aligning resources and outputs to contribute to a common goal of promoting gender equality, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability.
3. Ensuring that all projects are aligned with GO's funded project or programme objectives.

# 9 Conclusion and Recommendations

South Africa has made significant strides in developing the gender page of the plan. One of its notable achievements is the constitutional amendment to include gender equality, which is supported by legislation that promotes women's rights and participation in various spheres. The country has witnessed an increase in women representation in politics and decision-making positions.

However, despite these gains, there are still significant challenges that need to be addressed. These include the lack of integration of gender equality into the curriculum through high schools and tertiary institutions, as well as a lack of adequate resources for teaching gender equality. Additionally, educational opportunities for girls have expanded, contributing to a greater gender parity in schools and higher education.

Nonetheless, despite these achievements, challenges remain in ensuring full gender equality in South Africa. Gender-based violence remains a significant issue, with women facing high levels of sexual harassment and domestic abuse. Furthermore, women continue to face barriers in the workplace, including unequal pay, limited career advancement opportunities, and lack of representation in leadership positions.

Addressing these issues requires a comprehensive approach that includes the following:

- Integrating gender equality into the curriculum at all educational levels.
- Providing adequate resources for teaching gender equality.
- Ensuring equal access to education and training for women.
- Promoting women's participation in decision-making processes.
- Addressing the root causes of gender-based violence.

The government has made significant strides in addressing these issues, but more needs to be done. It is crucial to ensure that the gender page conditions are integrated into every stage of the Eco-Schools program from planning to evaluation, to create more effective and inclusive strategies that benefit all members of the community.

Addressing the gender page South Africa's need to continue to build on these achievements by strengthening the implementation of policies and programs aimed at promoting gender equality. This includes addressing the root causes of gender-based violence, ensuring equal access to education and training for women, and promoting women's participation in decision-making processes. Additionally, it is essential to provide adequate resources for teaching gender equality and to promote a culture of respect and equality.

The government has made significant strides in addressing these issues, but more needs to be done. It is crucial to ensure that the gender page conditions are integrated into every stage of the Eco-Schools program from planning to evaluation, to create more effective and inclusive strategies that benefit all members of the community.

# APPENDIX A Gender Assessment Checklist

## Guidelines

- **Read - Chicago**:
  - Read and understand the [Competency-Based Education (CBE) Guide](https://www.chicago.edu/academics/cbe).
  - Use the guide to assess how well the program aligns with CBE principles.
  - Note any areas where the program does not meet these standards.

- **Note**: Include the date when you completed this checklist and your name as a reference.

## Gender Equality Training Assessment

### Part A: The Standardized Tool Assessed - Course/Module (CM) Stage

1. The course/module includes:
   - GO (Gendered Outcomes) indicators.
   - GO indicators are appropriately used.
   - Actions are provided with the justification.

2. The course/module is designed and produced to include GO indicators for relevant outcomes.

The consultation process will ensure meaningful inclusion participation that engages industry, employers, and employees to inform the development of a national standard for inclusive employment planning and engagement practices. All stakeholders should provide individually unique plans, which outline their goals, objectives, and strategies to achieve the outcomes of the plan. The key principles that will underpin deliverable programs include:

- Ensuring ongoing quality and transparency in approach, ensuring inclusivity and sensitivity to diverse needs
- Closing the mechanisms for engagement and circulation throughout the entire alignment planning process
- Providing a framework for employers to develop and implement their own goals and objectives
- Ensuring adequate support is provided to assist individuals with disabilities to achieve their employment goals
- Ensuring adequate participation to relevant stakeholders, receiving and addressing diverse needs based on the individual's unique circumstances
- Strengthening visibility, engagement, participation, with ongoing local development planning and community engagement
- Continuously communicating and implementing these plans, considering any societal constraints, and ensuring that all individuals are included in the process
- Providing a framework for inclusive employment practices

Stakeholders will need to be regularly engaged project developments, updates, and community consultations. This will ensure new recurring reports and progress/achievements that inform the progress of the project and key deliverables.
